      "content": "\\section{Problem Setup and Methods}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}\n\\label{sec:commitment-point}\n\nWhen a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases; traces with oscillating safety deliberation are resolved by taking the final irreversible transition (\\cref{sec:limitations}). Steps before CP are \\emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \\emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The detection task produces a score $s_t \\in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.\n\nWe study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\\cref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Arch.} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Layers} & \\textbf{Hidden} & \\textbf{$n$ (total\\,/\\,test)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\\,/\\,43 \\\\\nOT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\\,/\\,47 \\\\\nR1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\\,/\\,20 \\\\\nQwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\\,/\\,19 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Probe Architecture}\n\\label{sec:probe-architecture}\n\n\\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\\mathbf{h}_t^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Sweeping $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields $<$2pp BA variation (\\cref{sec:experiments}), confirming robustness to this default. Layer selection is analyzed in \\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}.\n\n\\paragraph{Text probes.} Each step's text is encoded with all-MiniLM-L6-v2~\\citep{wang2020minilm} ($\\mathbb{R}^{384}$), a distilled sentence encoder from the Sentence-BERT family~\\citep{reimers2019sentence}. The same logistic regression is applied as a surface-level modality baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal windowing.} For window size $W$, we concatenate features from steps $[t{-}W{+}1, \\ldots, t]$. We evaluate $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ and select per-model on validation. GRU sequence models are also evaluated under two protocols: a 70/30 train/test split (primary) and the same 60/20/20 split used for LR experiments (for fair comparison; \\cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds,app:gru_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:layer-sensitivity}\n\nLayer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse modality comparison conclusions. We evaluate five metrics: balanced accuracy (BA), precision, and inverse FPR are standard probing metrics~\\citep{belinkov2022probing}; crossing rate and additive composite come from the safety detection literature. A multiplicative composite (BA $\\times$ crossing rate) serves as the \\emph{crossing-rate composite} (CR-composite) baseline, so named because it incorporates crossing rate in the selection criterion. Metrics evaluating probe performance at a fixed decision threshold (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) form the \\emph{threshold-based} selection. This classification is an empirical observation from metric disagreement, not an a priori design choice. Crossing rate inflates at shallow layers where representations are compressed (inter-step cosine distances 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller; \\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), an independently verifiable geometric property. Precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite all derive from the same $2\\times2$ confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same layer, consistent with their shared structure but not mathematically guaranteed (each metric defines a distinct optimization landscape over layers); BA is threshold-free and provides independent confirmation. The ablation diagnoses which \\emph{aspect} drives layer selection bias. The effective number of independent tests is four (one per model), reflected in our HB correction. Results in \\cref{sec:layer-results}; full per-layer sweeps in \\cref{app:layer_sweep}.\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Methods}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\nWe evaluate seven alternatives to contextualize probe performance (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian} and CUSUM~\\citep{page1954cusum} for change-point detection; Mahalanobis distance following TrajGuard~\\citep{trajguard}; RepE mean-difference directions~\\citep{zou2023representation}; two-layer MLP probes; CCS-adapted~\\citep{burns2023discovering}; and cross-model transfer via PCA. A position-only baseline ($t/T$) controls for temporal artifacts. Detailed configurations in \\cref{app:bocpd_config,app:transfer_details}.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\nWe report balanced accuracy, precision, pre-CP FPR, coverage, and lead time. We exclude F1 (conflates precision and recall), AUROC (threshold-free, cannot diagnose layer selection bias), and MCC (redundant with BA under balanced priors). Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings, selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed across all models as the value balancing coverage ($\\geq$70\\%) and safe-trace false positive rate ($\\leq$50\\%); \\cref{tab:k_ablation} confirms that the HS vs.\\ text conclusion direction is consistent at $K{=}3$, $5$, and $7$, with coverage and FPR trading off monotonically. Modality comparisons use BCa bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples, resampling traces as clusters: each trace is an independent unit and all its steps are included or excluded together, preserving within-trace dependence)~\\citep{efron1993bootstrap,diciccio1996bootstrap} with Holm-Bonferroni step-down (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$); the 4-test framework yields identical conclusions (\\cref{app:holm_bonferroni}). For 32B models ($n \\leq 20$), the exact permutation test is the primary inference method; BCa confidence intervals are reported for comparability but may have reduced coverage at these sample sizes (BCa assumes approximate pivotality; heavy-tailed score distributions could produce anti-conservative intervals). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated, so the correction is stricter than under independence. Control task selectivity~\\citep{hewitt2019control} validates genuine structure (\\cref{app:control}). $\\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \\leq 20$): R1-32B ($n{=}20$) and QwQ-32B ($n{=}19$).\n",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 51
    }

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related-work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}
4	Reasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}.
5	
6	\textit{Attack characterization.} Self-jailbreak has been documented with step-level annotations~\cite{harmthoughts}, with longer chains increasing circumvention probability~\cite{selfjailbreak}. H-CoT~\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked. Unfaithful reasoning~\cite{chen2025unfaithful,turpin2023language} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. Safety-aligned models maintain refusal intentions during thinking but experience a sharp drop at output generation~\cite{yin2025refusal_cliff,hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak,hu2026refusal_dynamics,ahrend2026safer_traces}.
7	
8	\textit{Detection.} CoT monitoring can detect reward hacking but optimization pressure promotes obfuscation~\cite{baker2025monitoring}. CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile~\cite{korbak2025cot_monitorability}. GuardReasoner~\cite{xiang2025guardreasoner} trains a dedicated reasoning-based guard model.
9	
10	\textit{Mitigation.} SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and Chain-of-Guardrails~\cite{mao2025selfjailbreak_reasoning} proposes guardrail insertion during reasoning. None of these conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}
13	Internal activations can detect safety violations in standard LLMs. \citet{azaria2023internal} demonstrate that internal states encode whether a model's output is truthful, motivating representation-based monitoring. \citet{zhou2024alignment} show safety-relevant information distributes across layers, while \citet{qi2025shallow_alignment} find that alignment operates primarily over early output tokens. \citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction, but \citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately. \citet{joad2026refusal_multi} reveal eleven category-specific refusal directions, and \citet{wollschlager2025geometry} formalize multi-dimensionality through concept cones, consistent with our finding that single-direction methods are insufficient. TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} uses Mahalanobis distance from safe trajectories; SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers; SIREN~\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation; SafeDream~\cite{safedream2026} proposes proactive world models for detection; HiddenDetect~\cite{jiang2025hiddendetect} extends hidden-state monitoring to vision-language models; SafeInt~\cite{safeint2025} applies representation intervention for defense; RCS~\cite{rcs2026} uses Mahalanobis-based contrastive scoring for jailbreak detection. These methods assume threats from external input manipulation and operate at the token level on instruction-tuned models. The self-jailbreak setting differs because transitions are internal and span multiple reasoning steps. \citet{kadali2025internal} decompose internal layer representations to separate jailbreak from benign prompts; their results confirm that layer-wise structure carries safety-relevant signal. Concurrently, \citet{chan2025probing} probe safety commitment in LLMs, complementing our focus on metric-induced layer selection bias. We reproduce TrajGuard (\cref{sec:negative}) and find its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal resides in minor PCA components.
14	
15	\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}
16	Causal abstraction~\cite{geiger2025causal} formalizes when high-level causal models faithfully describe neural computation, grounding use of internal representations. Linear probing spans layer-wise classifiers~\cite{alain2017understanding}, sentence probing~\cite{conneau2018probing}, and surveys~\cite{belinkov2019analysis,belinkov2022probing,rogers2020primer}. Control tasks~\cite{hewitt2019control} distinguish structure from memorization (we adopt these; \cref{app:control}). Information-theoretic and causal formulations address probe complexity~\cite{voita2020information,pimentel2020information,ravfogel2020null,ravfogel2022rlace,belrose2023leace,elazar2021amnesic}. \citet{marks2024geometry} find emergent linear structure in LLM truth representations, consistent with our safety commitment separability. That information distributes non-uniformly across layers is well established~\cite{tenney2019bert,jawahar2019bert}. Our contribution is orthogonal: the known layer-wise information gradient interacts with metric choice to produce systematic selection bias in safety probes. Most directly, \citet{boxo2025linear_probes} show linear probes can rely on textual evidence rather than deeper representations; our metric bias analysis provides a complementary mechanism: shallow-layer probes exploit compressed representations that inflate crossing rate, a geometric effect consistent with text-leakage at layers where representations have not yet diverged from surface features. \citet{alvarez2026transport_geometry} apply hidden-state geometry to step-level hallucination detection concurrently with our work. Despite this foundation, layer selection remains underexamined: most studies select by a single metric without sensitivity analysis, a practice we show can reverse conclusions (\cref{sec:layer-results}). \citet{pais2026linear_probe_scaling} show that multi-layer ensembling recovers performance where single-layer probes fail, a complementary solution to the sensitivity problem we diagnose. Our approach is correlational; causal validation~\citep{geiger2025causal,belrose2023leace,li2024iti} is needed to confirm probed directions are causally active.
17	
18	\paragraph{Alternative Detection Approaches.}
19	BOCPD~\cite{adams2007bayesian} detects abrupt distributional shifts; CCS~\cite{burns2023discovering} discovers latent truth directions via consistency constraints; RepE~\cite{zou2023representation} identifies concept directions through mean differences. We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection (\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and CUSUM fail because commitment is gradual. CCS provides no gain because its consistency objective misaligns with temporal structure. RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (0.756 on QwQ, 0.559 on R1-32B). These failures motivate supervised probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective methodology.
20	
```

I'm also adding requirements for replication and power analysis in the Discussion and Conclusion sections, and softening the 32B language in the final conclusion. I'm also adding a note about needing n ≥ 50 traces per model before making inferential claims about the 32B results. Let me map out where these changes need to go in the Abstract, Introduction, and other key sections. I'm working through the specific language changes needed across the document. I need to replace these with more cautious phrasing and add a replication requirement statement that clarifies we need at least 50 traces per model before drawing conclusions from the 32B findings.

For the detection framing, I'm distinguishing between what this work actually does—retrospective characterization of the phenomenon—versus the future goal of online detection systems. This distinction needs to appear early in the Introduction, clarifying that our methods aren't production-ready detectors given the false positive rates ranging from 11–50%.

I'm also planning to strengthen the position baseline methodology argument in the Discussion section, particularly around how linear and quadratic bracketing work and what the HS probes can actually tell us about the underlying mechanisms.

For the abstract, I need to add context to the MiniLM-384d comparison, noting that this advantage is conditional on the encoder choice and represents an upper bound rather than a general claim.

The Limitations section needs several additions: clarifying that the 12-test Holm-Bonferroni correction covers only the final comparisons and may underestimate Type I error; noting that three of four models share the Qwen architecture, so patterns might be Qwen-specific rather than universal; and replacing "confirmatory" with "cross-validated" throughout where it refers to the validation approach.

I also need to add a missing claim about the ensemble—it doesn't outperform oracle single-metric selection but removes the need to pre-select metrics. The $\\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \\leq 20$) where effect sizes are descriptive rather than confirmatory. Had we reported only CR-composite-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact. A random-label control on QwQ-32B confirms that the crossing-rate shallow-layer bias is a geometric property of the representations (100\\% of random-label repeats select shallow layers), while threshold-based deep-layer selection requires real safety labels (\\cref{app:random_cp}). AUROC, excluded from our primary metrics because it is threshold-free (\\cref{sec:evaluation}), confirms HS discriminability at BA-selected layers ($\\Delta$AUROC +12.1 to +15.1pp; \\cref{tab:ba_layer_results}) but cannot diagnose the threshold-dependent layer selection bias.\\looseness=-1",

          " \\paragraph{Position mediation.} Position ($t/T$) accounts for 9--20pp of balanced accuracy,\\footnote{R1-32B's 20.4pp drop reflects $W{=}1$; at $W{\\geq}3$, drops are 9--17pp.} but all models retain non-positional signal above chance (all $p{<}0.001$; \\cref{app:position_residual}). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$). Adding a quadratic position term $(t/T)^2$ does not change significance conclusions for the 7--8B models. However, QwQ-32B's advantage is fully absorbed by the quadratic term (\\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), suggesting its residualized advantage contains a non-linear position component. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).\\looseness=-1",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Caveats.} Nested 5-fold cross-validation confirms the HS precision advantage for both 7--8B models (10/10 folds; \\cref{app:nested_cv}), with shrinkage of 13--25\\% relative to holdout estimates, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. OT-7B nested CV selects L21--27 vs.\\ holdout L16 vs.\\ CR-composite L0; the +21.9pp holdout estimate is an upper bound, and the nested CV shrunk estimate is the conservative reference. This inconsistency itself supports Contribution~1 (metric-induced layer selection bias). R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\\% coverage;\\footnote{R1-32B's BA-optimal layer was identified post-hoc rather than through the pre-registered nested CV protocol used for 7--8B models, further motivating its exploratory status.} at $W{\\geq}3$, dimensionality prevents significance. Regularization $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp BA variation; decision thresholds are similarly stable (\\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps): higher precision at later detection. False alarm rates remain 11--50\\%, so current methods characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. Per-model details in \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}.",

This paper provides a systematic characterization of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect.",
    "newString": "Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; online detection remains a future goal.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before building production detectors, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides a systematic characterization of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion.\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{confirmatory} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV validated; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is confirmatory at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV validated), significant but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

    "oldString": "the HS advantage is confirmatory at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV validated), significant but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated).",
    "newString": "the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; online detection remains a future goal.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion.\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is confirmatory at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV validated), significant but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

    "newString": "This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; online detection remains a future goal.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion.\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

    "originalFile": "Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings. A three-layer ensemble provides metric-agnostic HS advantage matching the best individual metric (4/4 models, $p \\leq 0.002$).
